@@2396346 <h> Give geoengineering a chance to fix climate change : David Keith <h> If you want to see sparks fly , try suggesting geoengineering as a solution to global warming <p> As the term implies , geoengineering is engineering on a planetary scale . It involves attempting to arrest the course of climate change through mega-projects - by seeding the atmosphere with reflective particles , for example , or by putting gigantic mirrors in orbit around the Earth to reflect sunlight back to space , or by fertilizing the ocean with iron to stimulate the growth of carbon-absorbing plankton . <p> There may indeed be broad agreement among scientists that climate change is happening , humans are causing it and urgent action is needed to prevent a global disaster . New reports from the American Association for the Advancement of Science and the United Nations Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change only add to the weight of science 's verdict on the subject . Just what to do about climate change , however - and how quickly - is still a matter of intense political and policy debate . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fly , try suggesting geoengineering as a solution to global warming . <p> David Keith took first prize in Canada 's national physics prize exam , won MIT 's prize for excellence in experimental physics , and was listed as one of TIME magazine 's Heroes of the Environment for 2009 . A proponent of geoengineering to address climate change , he 's now a professor of applied physics and of public policy at Harvard University . ( Courtesy Harvard ) <p> ? As the term implies , geoengineering is engineering on a planetary scale . <p> Geoengineering is an attempt to arrest the course of climate change through a number of different schemes , such as seeding the atmosphere with reflective particles . Or putting gigantic mirrors in orbit around the Earth to reflect sunlight back to space . Or fertilizing the ocean with iron to stimulate the growth of carbon-absorbing plankton . <p> For a lot of people , it sounds like mad science . <p> And geoengineering has been a magnet for controversy and criticism . Its opponents include some of the world 's most prominent environmentalists , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ year , in fact , the former U.S. Vice President said the very idea of geoengineering is " insane , utterly mad and delusional in the extreme . " He added , " the fact that some scientists who should know better are actually engaged in serious discussion of those alternatives is a mark of how desperate some of them are feeling due to the paralysis in the global political system . " <p> Jonathan Schell tribute : The expert on the politics and morality of nuclear weapons died this week . <p> Spare Change : For many of us , panhandlers provide a daily prick to the conscience . <p> The Centenary of the Newfoundland Sealing Disaster : Poet and novelist Michael Crummey and author Jenny Higgins , on the 1914 tragedy in which 79 sealers froze to death on the ice floes off Newfoundland . <p> But Canadian environmental engineer David Keith is taken seriously by policymakers and scientists when he speaks about the possibilities of geoengineering . <p> Keith was a long-time professor at the University of Calgary and is now a Professor of Public Policy at the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Applied Sciences at Harvard University . He 's particularly interested in solar geoengineering , or solar radiation management , which would involve putting tiny sulphur particles into the stratosphere , where they would reflect solar energy back to space . <p> In his new book , A Case for Climate Engineering , Keith says geoengineering is a " brutally ugly technical fix . " He cheerfully admits he has a lot of qualms about it as a technology that could have dangerous and unintended consequences , and that it does n't address the root cause of climate change : the buildup of greenhouse gases in the atmosphere . <p> But , as Keith told The Sunday Edition 's Michael Enright in an interview , that does n't mean we should ignore the fact that it could rapidly lower the Earth 's temperature and counteract some of the effects of climate change . <p> It 's technically feasible and relatively inexpensive to do , he adds . <p> And given how the global community has dragged its heels on reducing emissions , he argues , a crude , quick fix for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> " I think the important point is that it 's not hard to do , that all the hard questions are about whether we should do it , who controls it , how well it works . " <p> Keith also acknowledges the danger that if geoengineering were to become seen as a proven solution to rising global temperatures , there would be a strong temptation to forego costly emissions reductions and simply press ahead with geoengineering to counteract the results of rising levels of greenhouse gases in the atmosphere instead . <p> " You need to do geoengineering during the time that you slow down emissions . In the long run , you have to bring emissions to zero , " he says . <p> According to Keith , if we want a stable climate , we must eventually stop putting more carbon dioxide in the atmosphere . " Carbon dioxide is like filling a bathtub . The climate risk comes from the historical sum of all emissions . The only way to stop adding to that risk is to stop putting more carbon dioxide in . " <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ carbon dioxide emissions over 100 years . If you do this solar geoengineering , you could spread out the climate change over 200 years , slowing down the amount of climate change , and I would say most climate risks have to do with the rate of change . " <p> Keith is not calling for an immediate adoption of geoengineering . What he wants to see is scientific and political energy poured into research into geoengineering 's possibilities and risks , and a robust public debate so that informed policy decisions can be made about whether it 's a viable tool to prevent the worst effects of climate change . <p> You can hear Michael Enright 's entire conversation with David Keith this weekend in Hour 2 of The Sunday Edition , which begins at 10:05 a.m. on CBC Radio One , or in the audio-player link at the top-left of this page . <p> Note : The CBC does not necessarily endorse any of the views posted . By submitting your comments , you acknowledge that CBC has the right to reproduce , broadcast and publicize those comments or @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that comments are moderated and published according to our submission guidelines .

@@2397492 <p> From small islands to big continents , from the tropics to the poles , climate change is already leaving its mark , damaging crops , spreading diseases and acidifying oceans , says a new blockbuster UN climate science report released in Japan on Monday . <p> The assessment by the United Nations-sponsored Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change also warns that governments are ill-prepared and if action is not taken soon , risks could become unmanageable . <p> Asia , the report says , will bear the brunt of climate change : experts predict the displacement of millions of people from low-lying coastal areas , conflicts between growing populations and diminishing resources , the wiping of trillions of dollars from economies , and even the increasing risk of heat-related deaths . <p> The consequences of many of these risks could be lowered if action is taken to curb greenhouse gas emissions that cause climate change , the report says . But it also recognizes that a certain amount of warming can not be reversed , and , in some cases , there is no escaping its impact . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ change , " Vicente Barros , co-chair of the group that prepared the report , said in a statement . " In many cases , we are not prepared for the climate-related risks that we already face . Investments in better preparation can pay dividends both for the present and for the future . " <p> This is the second of three reports on the causes , impacts and solutions to climate change . <p> Scientists , government representatives and observers -- more than 500 people from 115 countries -- butted heads in Yokohama , Japan , for a week before signing this report . <p> In September , the first IPCC report warned that pollution from burning fossil fuels is changing the earth 's climate and contributing to rising seas , stronger storms , hotter days and severe droughts . <p> There is potential for adaptation , said Paul Kovacs , executive director of the Institute for Catastrophic Loss Reduction in Toronto and one of the lead authors of the part of the report focusing on North America , including Canada . <p> " It is not the complete answer @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ adding that adaptation can reduce the adverse impact of climate change somewhat even if it ca n't eliminate risks from extreme weather . <p> " Climate has changed and further change is inevitable . It is coming , " said Kovacs . " Extreme events are one of the key parts that demonstrate how vulnerable we are and our capacity to manage and cope better with resources going forward . " <p> Much of the adaptation , he said , has been reactive , meaning that it occurs after events , particularly after extreme events , " but adaptation is beginning . " <p> For the first time , the report also made a connection between the impacts of a warming world and global conflicts . It does n't say that climate change is causing or will cause strife , but that it can " indirectly increase risks of violent conflicts ... " <p> Fights over resources , responses to extreme weather and migration patterns could all play a role in destabilizing the world a bit more , it says . <p> The connection between global warming and violent confrontation is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ an American think-tank argued that climate change played a significant role in the Arab Spring . @@2399217 <h> Province and Port of Vancouver back down and settle truckers strike <p> The lengthy strike by truck drivers at the Port of Vancouver BC who move half of the port 's shipping containers has been settled . An agreement was reached on March 26 that will see an increase of 12 per cent to the rate paid to truckers to move each container . <p> The contentious issue of waiting times to pick up or offload shipments is also addressed in the agreement . Measures will be taken to reduce waiting times , and truckers will be paid modest amounts for the time they are kept waiting to load or offload . <p> The agreement covers some 250 truckers who drive company-owned rigs and are members of the Unifor union , and more than 1,000 owner-operator , ' independent ' truckers . <p> As negotiations came to a head , the British Columbia government withdrew a threat to order the unionized truckers back to work . For reasons that are unclear , the federal fobbed the matter off to its provincial partner . <p> Vancouver is Canada 's largest ocean port . The news of the settlement is not surprising . The Port of Vancouver 's aggressive stand towards the truckers was a public relations fiasco waiting to unravel because the port has n't granted per-container rate increases to truckers since 2005 . Following the signing of the agreement , Unifor National President Jerry Dias said that unionized truckers were prepared to defy a back to work order . Independent truckers had said they would not return to work under the slave labour rates to which the Port was apparently sticking . <p> Another impetus to the agreement is the great rush with which the fossil fuel industry in Canada and BC is pressing to expand the extraction and export of tar sands bitumen , coal and liquefied natural gas . @@2399432 <p> AYABE , JAPAN -- Every time Masatoshi Kusakari picked up a sack of rice or a head of lettuce in a local grocery store on the outskirts of Tokyo , the words played over in his mind . <p> " They ( the government ) were on radio and TV and in newspapers saying , ' Do n't worry , our food supply is safe , there is no danger , ' " said Kusakari , 33 , a calligrapher and art teacher . Instead , they eat imported fruits and vegetables and produce grown indoors using hydroponics . <p> " We have tried the hydroponic lettuce . It 's good , " said Kusakari , who last year moved his family from Tokyo to the countryside outside Kyoto because he was worried the capital city was too close to the Fukushima Dai-ichi power plant , which is still leaking radioactive waste . <p> Three years after the tsunami killed @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's move and their continued skepticism of locally grown produce highlight the crisis facing Japan 's agricultural sector . It is dying . <p> Even before the tsunami sent public confidence in Japan 's food tumbling , the country struggled to feed its 127 million residents . Japan is the world 's largest net importer of food , bringing in 16 times more than it exports , according to the Organization for Economic Co-operation and Development . After importing 21 per of its food in 1960 , Japan now imports 61 per cent . And that figure is rising . <p> Fewer young Japanese want to pursue farming because most farms are small and inefficient . The average age of Japanese farmers is 70 . Of the 1.5 million who still work the land , only 420,000 do so full-time . <p> " We will not have an agricultural sector to speak of in future generations , " said Yoshiisa Godo , an economics professor at Meiji Gakuin University in Tokyo . " This has become our destiny . <p> " Our government realizes what is happening and to try to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ young farmers in the same way it idolized young soldiers during the Pacific War , but it 's too little too late . " <p> The tsunami may have helped Japan find a solution , which could one day change the way people around the world get food . The fix is to grow vegetables indoors , in factories , without soil . <p> Japan has at least 150 hydroponic plant factories -- more than anywhere in the world -- where food is grown in temperature- and humidity-controlled buildings under fluorescent lights . <p> The Babylonians are said to have used the technique in their hanging gardens 2,500 years ago . The U.S. occupying force in Japan after the Second World War used hydroponics because some farmers were using human excrement as fertilizer , according to Toru Maruo , a professor of vegetable sciences at Chiba University in Tokyo . <p> " Indoor plant farming is the solution in Japan , " said Dickson Despommier , a professor of public health at Columbia University who wrote The Vertical Farm : Feeding the World in the 21st Century . The UN agency is expected to warn on March 31 that climate change will lead to declines in global agricultural yields of as much as 2 per cent each decade . <p> In India , poised to become the world 's most populous country , water tables are drying up , which could slash agricultural output by 40 per cent over the next 70 years .
